Framing the Magic Box	Introduce an unfamiliar concept	You have a named framework or unique method
Handling Objections	Remove reasons to lose interest	Your audience is likely to have a specific concern
Bold Claim Double-Down	Reinforce understanding	Your opening claim is unusual or unclear

You don't have to use all six techniques in every video.

Choose the approach that best strengthens your opening.

You can also combine multiple techniques when they naturally complement one another.

For example, you could introduce a unique framework, reinforce it with a desirable transformation, and then provide evidence that it works.

The important thing is to avoid making the opening unnecessarily complicated.

Every additional sentence should have a purpose.
4. Final Takeaway
A strong video opening is not just about writing an attention-grabbing first sentence.

What happens immediately afterwards matters just as much.

The lock-in zone helps you turn initial interest into stronger curiosity by giving viewers additional reasons to continue watching.

Remember the six techniques:
1. Teasing the Proof: Support your opening promise with genuine evidence.
2. Desirable Transformation: Show the positive change viewers can expect.
3. Introducing Contrast: Compare familiar approaches with a different possibility.
4. Framing the Magic Box: Give an interesting method or framework a memorable name.
5. Handling Objections: Address the main concern that might make viewers scroll away.
6. Bold Claim Double-Down: Reinforce an important statement so viewers understand it clearly.
Ultimately, the purpose of the lock-in zone is to make your opening more convincing, more relevant, and more difficult to abandon.

A hook may earn you the first few seconds of someone's attention, but a well-constructed lock-in zone gives them a stronger reason to stay for the rest of the video.





1,000 Original Hooks and Rehooks
A practical example bank for short-form videos, educational content, tutorials, storytelling, business content, and creator-led videos.
How to use this library
- A hook earns attention and makes the subject, stakes, or benefit clear.
- A rehook follows the opening and gives viewers a fresh reason to keep watching.
- Keep the spoken line, on-screen text, and opening visual aligned around one central idea.
- Avoid promising a result the video does not deliver. The next few seconds should confirm the hook.
- Treat these as starting points. Add a specific audience, situation, demonstration, or proof.
- A rehook should advance the thought, deepen curiosity, introduce contrast, handle an objection, or preview the next useful step.

Quick pairing examples
Pairing 1
- Hook: Most people approach learning a new skill backwards. Here is a better way to make steady progress without guessing.
- Rehook: Now here is where people usually go wrong: they confuse activity with progress.
Pairing 2
- Hook: If you are posting often without being remembered, this one shift in building a personal brand can help you become recognizable to the right audience.
- Rehook: Keep that in mind, because the next part changes how you should approach building a personal brand.
Pairing 3
- Hook: You do not need fancy editing to rescue an unclear idea; you need to understand a tighter opening.
- Rehook: Here is the twist: the obvious solution may be making the problem worse.
Final reminder
The strongest opening is not necessarily the loudest or most dramatic. It makes the right viewer understand why the subject matters, creates a meaningful reason to continue, and then delivers on that expectation.

# Section B: Connector Rules & Transition Language
---
file: connectors
scope: core
applies_to: all_niches
version: 1.0
---

# Connector Rules — Transition Language

## What a Connector Is

A connector is one sentence — sometimes just a phrase — that sits between
two sections of a script. Its only job is to make the viewer choose to stay
for the next beat. It is not a summary of what just happened. It is not a
heading. It is a pull forward.

Think of it as the cliff-hanger at the end of a TV episode. Small, specific,
and it makes leaving feel like a loss.

---

## The One Rule That Overrides Everything

**If the connector does not make the viewer need to hear what comes next,
cut it. A clean cut is always better than a weak bridge.**

Silence, a pause, or a direct jump to the next idea is stronger than a
filler transition. Weak connectors are the number one reason viewers tap
away between beats.

---

## The Three Jobs a Connector Can Do

Every connector must do exactly one of these. If it does none, delete it.

**1. Open a question the viewer does not yet have the answer to**
The viewer's brain stays engaged because it is now looking for the answer.

**2. Create a gap between what the viewer knows and what they want to know**
You hint that something is missing from their understanding. They stay to
fill the gap.

**3. Signal that a payoff is coming in the very next sentence**
You make a specific promise that the next beat will deliver on immediately.
Do not promise and then delay. If you say "here is the fix," the fix must
be the next sentence.

---

## Approved Connector Patterns

These are structures, not scripts. Write them in your own voice.
Do not paste these word for word into a script.

### Pattern 1 — The Question Bridge
Closes a beat by asking the question the next beat answers.

Examples:
- "But here is the part nobody talks about."
- "So why does this keep happening?"
- "And that is exactly where it falls apart."
- "The question is — what do you do with that?"

### Pattern 2 — The Contrast Shift
Sets up a tension between two ideas. Works well between a common belief
and a reframe.

Examples:
- "That works for established brands. It does not work for you — yet."
- "On paper that makes sense. In practice it is the opposite."
- "Most people hear this and stop. The ones who win keep going."
- "Sounds simple. It is not. Here is why."

### Pattern 3 — The Specific Promise
Names exactly what is coming next. The specificity is what creates trust
and keeps the viewer watching.

Examples:
- "There is one shift that changes all of this. It takes about ten seconds
  to understand."
- "Here is what actually works — and I will show you in the next thirty
  seconds."
- "There are two reasons this happens. The second one is the one that
  matters."

### Pattern 4 — The Reframe Bridge
Redefines what the viewer just heard before moving to the next idea.
Used when you want the viewer to hold the previous beat differently.

Examples:
- "So it is not really about the content. It is about what the content
  signals."
- "The tool is not the problem. This is."
- "That is not a strategy problem. That is a clarity problem."

### Pattern 5 — The Unexpected Turn
Works when the next beat contradicts or complicates what came before.
Creates curiosity by making the viewer feel the previous beat was
incomplete.

Examples:
- "Except that is not the full picture."
- "Here is where it gets interesting."
- "And then everything I just said has one exception."

---

## Connectors to Never Use

These phrases slow the script down and signal to the viewer that nothing
important is coming. Remove every single one on sight.

- "So anyway..."
- "Moving on..."
- "The next thing I want to talk about is..."
- "As I mentioned earlier..."
- "Let me explain..."
- "With that being said..."
- "So basically..."
- "In conclusion..."
- "To summarise..."
- Any connector that starts with "I" — it shifts focus from the viewer
  to the creator
- Any connector longer than fifteen words
- Any rhetorical question where the viewer already knows the answer
  ("Pretty important, right?")
- Filler affirmations ("Right, so..." / "OK so..." / "Yeah so...")

---

## Placement Rules

- **One connector** between the Hook and the first Body beat — maximum
- **One connector** between Body beats — only when the jump between ideas
  is large enough to need it
- **Zero connectors** before the Close. Cut directly into it. The momentum
  of the body carries the viewer to the close on its own
- Never stack two connectors back to back. If you feel the need to,
  the problem is the body beat structure, not the connector
- In scripts under 45 seconds: aim for one connector total, possibly zero
- In scripts 60–120 seconds: two connectors maximum

---

## Connector Length by Script Duration

| Script length     | Max connector length |
|-------------------|----------------------|
| Under 30 seconds  | 5 words              |
| 30–60 seconds     | 8 words              |
| 60–120 seconds    | 12 words             |

If you need more words than this to transition, the previous beat did not
land cleanly. Fix the beat, not the connector.

---

## Tone Matching

A connector must feel like it belongs in the same conversation as the rest
of the script. If the script is direct and confident, the connector must be
direct and confident. If the niche file sets a warmer tone, the connector
softens to match.

The connector is not where you change gear. It is where you hold gear.

Each niche pack will specify vocabulary preferences for connectors in that
niche. When a niche rule conflicts with a core rule, the niche rule wins.

---

## Side-by-Side Examples

### Example 1

**WEAK**
> [Body beat 1 ends]
> "So moving on to the next point, I want to talk about consistency and why
> it matters for your brand."
> [Body beat 2 begins]

**STRONG**
> [Body beat 1 ends]
> "And consistency is where most people quit — right before it starts
> working."
> [Body beat 2 begins]

---

### Example 2

**WEAK**
> [Hook ends]
> "Let me explain why this is so important for content creators like you."
> [Body beat 1 begins]

**STRONG**
> [Hook ends]
> "Here is what nobody tells you about why."
> [Body beat 1 begins]

---

### Example 3

**WEAK**
> [Body beat 2 ends]
> "With that being said, let us wrap this up."
> [Close begins]

**STRONG**
> [Body beat 2 ends — Close begins immediately, no connector]

---


## How to Read These Examples

Every example is built from a real handoff between two sections in the branding and marketing transcripts (Transcripts 1–15, in order). Each one is a standalone option, not a sequence: in a real script you would use one connector between the Hook and Body 1 at most, and one between Body beats only when the jump is large.

Every STRONG connector is tagged with the guide rules it follows:

- **Placement** — Hook → Body 1, or Body → Body (never before the Close)
- **Pattern** — one of the five approved patterns in `connectors.md`
- **Job** — the one job it does: open a question, create a gap, or signal an immediate payoff
- **Length tier** — the maximum words allowed for the script duration it fits

Each STRONG connector is a single sentence or phrase that does not recap the previous beat, and it never starts with "I". These are structures to learn from, so write your own in your own voice rather than pasting them.

---

`Hook → Body 1` · Pattern 3 — Specific Promise · Job 3 — signals an immediate payoff · 30–60s (≤8 words) · 7 words

## When the Best Connector Is None

The guide's override rule: if a connector does not make the viewer need what comes next, cut it. Before the Close there are zero connectors, because the momentum of the body carries the viewer in.

---

### Example 70
*Transcript 9 — Pre-production questions → Close*
`Body → Close` · No connector

**WEAK**
> [Final body beat ends]
> "That covers the main ideas, so here's how to wrap up."
> [Close begins]

**STRONG**
> [Pre-production questions end — Close begins immediately, no connector]

---

### Example 71
*Transcript 13 — The three paths → Close*
`Body → Close` · No connector

**WEAK**
> [Final body beat ends]
> "With the paths explained, it's time to close this out."
> [Close begins]

**STRONG**
> [Final path ends — Close begins immediately, no connector]

---

### Example 72
*Transcript 14 — Commitment Three → Close*
`Body → Close` · No connector

**WEAK**
> [Final body beat ends]
> "That brings us to the end of the commitments."
> [Close begins]

**STRONG**
> [Commitment Three ends — Close begins immediately, no connector]

---

## What the Strong Versions Have in Common

| Pattern | What It Does | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Question Bridge** | Closes a beat by asking the question the next beat answers | 15 |
| **Contrast Shift** | Sets two ideas against each other so the next beat resolves the tension | 6, 7, 8, 13, 32, 44, 58 |
| **Specific Promise** | Names exactly what is coming, which creates trust | 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 10, 11, 14, 16, 18, 24, 25, 26, 30, 31, 34, 38, 40, 42, 45, 47, 49, 50, 55, 56, 57, 60, 61, 64, 66, 68 |
| **Reframe Bridge** | Changes how the viewer holds what they just heard | 19, 22, 23, 27, 36, 43, 48, 51, 63 |
| **Unexpected Turn** | Makes the previous beat feel incomplete | 9, 12, 17, 20, 21, 28, 29, 33, 35, 37, 39, 41, 46, 52, 53, 54, 59, 62, 65, 67, 69 |


---

## Final Check Before Locking a Script

Read every connector out loud. Ask one question for each:

> **"If I cut this sentence, does the viewer lose anything they need?"**

If the answer is no — cut it.


---

# Section C: 50 Core Formats, 10 Story Angles & Format Executions
# 500 branding and marketing format executions

The format executions below are derived from the content forms and marketing practices in the transcripts. They combine 50 core formats with 10 story angles. Each execution includes two short-story examples, for 500 format executions and 1,000 examples total.

## 50 core formats

1. **Short-form vertical problem–solution video** — A fast social video that opens with a recognizable need and shows a useful response.
2. **FAQ or objection-response clip** — A direct answer to one real customer question or purchase hesitation.
3. **Step-by-step tutorial** — A practical walkthrough for one task or use case.
4. **Educational carousel** — A swipeable lesson that moves from question to explanation and action.
5. **Static story-led ad** — A single image or graphic that carries a tension, value, and proof point.
6. **Customer testimonial interview** — A real customer describes a need, decision, and experience.
7. **Before-and-after sequence** — A documented change shown with context about what changed.
8. **Customer case study** — A deeper narrative about context, choice, implementation, and verified results.
9. **Review or quote creative** — An approved review or quote presented with enough context to interpret it.
10. **Customer journey timeline** — A visual map from first need through decision, use, and follow-up.
11. **Founder-origin story** — The observation or problem that led to creating an offer.
12. **Expert explainer** — A knowledgeable person makes a complex idea clear and useful.
13. **Founder or employee Q&A** — A direct question reveals a decision, value, or lesson from the team.
14. **Podcast or interview clip** — A longer conversation edited around one memorable insight or story.
15. **About-page or brand-story article** — A written narrative explaining purpose, choices, values, and audience.
16. **Product demonstration** — The product is shown in use so people can understand how it works.
17. **Unboxing or reveal video** — A first look at a product or package, emphasizing discovery and details.
18. **Behind-the-scenes process video** — A production, preparation, or service step is made visible.
19. **Product-detail or macro showcase** — Close views of material, construction, texture, or function.
20. **Product-page narrative** — A product page answers what it is, who it suits, and why it is valuable.
21. **Street interview** — Short public questions surface needs, opinions, or reactions.
22. **Employee-generated story** — A staff member shares a real work moment, customer question, or insight.
23. **UGC creator video** — Creator-led content explains a product or service in a native, personal style.
24. **Creator partnership or advocacy series** — Repeated creator appearances build familiarity and sustained trust.
25. **Community event recap** — A gathering is told through people, moments, learning, and shared experience.
26. **High-production campaign film** — A polished visual story uses deliberate setting, pacing, and art direction.
27. **Low-fi native social clip** — Informal direct-to-camera content that feels natural in a social feed.
28. **Day-in-the-life vlog** — A routine or workday reveals decisions, process, and point of view.
29. **Recurring branded series** — A repeatable episode structure makes the content recognizable over time.
30. **Social show or episodic format** — A recurring show uses a host, guests, questions, or audience participation.
31. **Fair product comparison** — Options are compared by use case, features, tradeoffs, and customer needs.
32. **Myth-busting video** — A common assumption is examined with explanation and evidence.
33. **Social-proof montage** — Authentic comments, use cases, or endorsements are grouped around a need.
34. **Alternative breakdown guide** — A guide compares different approaches and when each one makes sense.
35. **Live demonstration or stream** — A real-time walkthrough answers audience questions as they arise.
36. **Launch countdown or reveal** — A staged sequence introduces a launch through clues, details, and reveal.
37. **Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign** — A timely story connects the offer to a relevant season or shared moment.
38. **Challenge or contest activation** — An audience prompt invites participation around a relevant task.
39. **Product-sampling story** — A sample is introduced in a relevant setting and followed by honest reactions.
40. **Brand-collaboration story** — Two brands or disciplines come together around a shared audience or idea.
41. **Storytime vignette** — A compact personal or customer-inspired narrative has a clear turn.
42. **Green-screen commentary** — A speaker reacts to a relevant image, claim, or trend with added context.
43. **One-shot narrative video** — A continuous visual sequence follows a single action or reveal.
44. **Situational skit** — A relatable scene dramatizes a common misunderstanding or friction.
45. **Template or trend adaptation** — A familiar format is adapted to express a genuine brand or product difference.
46. **Welcome or onboarding email** — A first message helps a new customer understand what to expect and do next.
47. **Promotional email or offer narrative** — An offer is presented through a customer need, clear value, and simple terms.
48. **Newsletter or editorial recommendation** — A useful recommendation is explained through a point of view and relevant context.
49. **Landing-page case narrative** — A page moves from customer context to solution, evidence, and action.
50. **Sales-enablement customer-proof deck** — A concise story helps a buyer explain a decision and evidence internally.

## Story angles

1. **Problem-to-solution** — Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.
2. **Origin and purpose** — Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.
3. **Mechanism and how it works** — Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.
4. **Objection and proof** — Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.
5. **Comparison and tradeoff** — Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.
6. **Transformation and journey** — Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.
7. **Craft and detail** — Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.
8. **Identity and community** — Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.
9. **Launch, offer, and timing** — Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.
10. **Feedback and iteration** — Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

Examples are adaptable story prompts, not claims about real customers. Use verified details and customer permission for testimonials, reviews, endorsements, results, and before-and-after content.

# Format executions and examples

## Core format 1: Short-form vertical problem–solution video

### 001. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A first-time shopper considering an everyday apparel item faces uncertainty about which option suits them; the story uses a one-page comparison guide to make one practical next step clear.
2. A busy parent considering a creative service faces a repeated manual task; the story uses a transparent pricing breakdown to make one practical next step clear.

### 002. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a page that hides key details from a new team member while they consider a personal-care product; that observation inspires a return-policy summary and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about a crowded shelf with no comparison cues from a community organizer while they consider a retail display; that observation inspires a side-by-side use case and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 003. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A product designer wants to select a suitable product version; a demonstration of a small-business software tool uses a visual label to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A prospective client wants to prepare for a launch; a demonstration of an onboarding program uses a close-up product detail to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 004. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A subscription member questions whether a crafted home object fits their needs; evidence such as a transparent demonstration under stated conditions provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A small-business owner questions whether an everyday apparel item fits their needs; evidence such as a case example shared with permission provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 005. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A first-time shopper compares a fitness accessory with a different feature set; a guide to repair and care helps explain which option is better suited to a daily commute.
2. A busy parent compares a personal-care product with a quick fix; a guide to the context where each works best helps explain which option is better suited to travel.

### 006. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A new team member starts with a content plan that changes every week; the story follows how clear care instructions change one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A community organizer starts with a search that returns too many choices; the story follows how a sample or trial changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 007. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A designer shows one detail of a community membership; the maker uses a live demo to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a price they cannot evaluate.
2. A service lead shows one detail of a crafted home object; the maker uses a short demonstration to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a maintenance step they keep forgetting.

### 008. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Identity and community

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of returning customers, a subscription member shares a repeated manual task; the group uses checklist to turn a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of people comparing products, a small-business owner shares a purchase requiring multiple approvals; a community Q&A turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 009. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a specialty food item is introduced, the team previews a real customer question; a first-time shopper gets a clear answer to a crowded shelf with no comparison cues and knows what to expect.
2. Before a customer-support service is introduced, the team previews an expert walkthrough; a busy parent gets a clear answer to conflicting advice and knows what to expect.

### 010. Short-form vertical problem–solution video × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Hook visually in the opening seconds; show the friction, the product or process, and one takeaway. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a live-event question from a new team member, the team revisits a learning program; the team uses a step-by-step sequence to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a recurring customer objection from a community organizer, the team revisits a community membership; the team uses a one-page comparison guide to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 2: FAQ or objection-response clip

### 011. FAQ or objection-response clip × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A retail associate considering a learning program faces a maintenance step they keep forgetting; the story uses clear care instructions to make one practical next step clear.
2. A product designer considering a community membership faces uncertainty about which option suits them; the story uses a sample or trial to make one practical next step clear.

### 012. FAQ or objection-response clip × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a purchase requiring multiple approvals from a commuter while they consider a subscription offer; that observation inspires a live demo and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about a page that hides key details from a subscription member while they consider a home-storage product; that observation inspires a short demonstration and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 013. FAQ or objection-response clip × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A cautious buyer wants to make a more informed choice; a demonstration of a beauty product uses a shared checklist to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A first-time shopper wants to select a suitable product version; a demonstration of a specialty food item uses a community Q&A to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 014. FAQ or objection-response clip × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A gift shopper questions whether a premium product fits their needs; evidence such as a clear returns policy and support path provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A new team member questions whether a learning program fits their needs; evidence such as photographs showing the actual finish provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 015. FAQ or objection-response clip × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A retail associate compares a seasonal product with a simplified version; a guide to time and setup helps explain which option is better suited to beginner use.
2. A product designer compares a subscription offer with a do-it-yourself approach; a guide to features the customer actually needs helps explain which option is better suited to a shared workspace.

### 016. FAQ or objection-response clip × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A commuter starts with a routine they keep postponing; the story follows how a customer feedback session changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A subscription member starts with a content plan that changes every week; the story follows how a return-policy summary changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 017. FAQ or objection-response clip × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service lead shows one detail of a service package; the maker uses a transparent pricing breakdown to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses an unclear return or delivery policy.
2. A product team shows one detail of a premium product; the maker uses a visual label to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a price they cannot evaluate.

### 018. FAQ or objection-response clip × Identity and community

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of local shop owners, a gift shopper shares uncertainty about which option suits them; a side-by-side use case turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of first-time founders, a new team member shares a repeated manual task; a prototype review turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 019. FAQ or objection-response clip × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a creative service is introduced, the team previews a close-up product detail; a retail associate gets a clear answer to a page that hides key details and knows what to expect.
2. Before a digital planning tool is introduced, the team previews a mobile-page rewrite; a product designer gets a clear answer to a crowded shelf with no comparison cues and knows what to expect.

### 020. FAQ or objection-response clip × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Put the question on screen, answer plainly, then show evidence or a next step. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a return reason from a commuter, the team revisits a retail display; the team uses an annotated quick-start card to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a repeated help-center search from a subscription member, the team revisits a service package; the team uses clear care instructions to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 3: Step-by-step tutorial

### 021. Step-by-step tutorial × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service professional considering a retail display faces a price they cannot evaluate; the story uses a customer feedback session to make one practical next step clear.
2. A cautious buyer considering a service package faces a maintenance step they keep forgetting; the story uses a return-policy summary to make one practical next step clear.

### 022. Step-by-step tutorial × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a repeated manual task from a workshop maker while they consider an onboarding program; that observation inspires a transparent pricing breakdown and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about a purchase requiring multiple approvals from a gift shopper while they consider a reusable travel accessory; that observation inspires a visual label and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 023. Step-by-step tutorial × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A local shop owner wants to use a new feature correctly; a demonstration of an everyday apparel item uses a side-by-side use case to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A retail associate wants to make a more informed choice; a demonstration of a creative service uses a prototype review to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 024. Step-by-step tutorial × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A returning customer questions whether a personal-care product fits their needs; evidence such as reviews that include both strengths and tradeoffs provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A commuter questions whether a retail display fits their needs; evidence such as published product details and limitations provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 025. Step-by-step tutorial × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service professional compares a small-business software tool with a quick fix; a guide to repair and care helps explain which option is better suited to a busy season.
2. A cautious buyer compares an onboarding program with a low-cost option; a guide to the context where each works best helps explain which option is better suited to a repeat routine.

### 026. Step-by-step tutorial × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A workshop maker starts with a launch message that assumes too much; the story follows how a design mockup changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A gift shopper starts with a routine they keep postponing; the story follows how a live demo changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 027. Step-by-step tutorial × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A product team shows one detail of a fitness accessory; the maker uses a sample or trial to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a search that returns too many choices.
2. A support teammate shows one detail of a personal-care product; the maker uses a shared checklist to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses an unclear return or delivery policy.

### 028. Step-by-step tutorial × Identity and community

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of people building better routines, a returning customer shares a maintenance step they keep forgetting; a short demonstration turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of busy families, a commuter shares uncertainty about which option suits them; a real customer question turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 029. Step-by-step tutorial × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a community membership is introduced, the team previews a community Q&A; a service professional gets a clear answer to a purchase requiring multiple approvals and knows what to expect.
2. Before a crafted home object is introduced, the team previews a step-by-step sequence; a cautious buyer gets a clear answer to a page that hides key details and knows what to expect.

### 030. Step-by-step tutorial × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Show each step in order and make the first action easy to follow. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a support question about the first step from a workshop maker, the team revisits a home-storage product; the team uses an expert walkthrough to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a team member's workflow note from a gift shopper, the team revisits a fitness accessory; the team uses a customer feedback session to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 4: Educational carousel

### 031. Educational carousel × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A student considering a home-storage product faces an unclear return or delivery policy; the story uses a design mockup to make one practical next step clear.
2. A local shop owner considering a fitness accessory faces a price they cannot evaluate; the story uses a live demo to make one practical next step clear.

### 032. Educational carousel × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about uncertainty about which option suits them from a team manager while they consider a specialty food item; that observation inspires a sample or trial and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about a repeated manual task from a returning customer while they consider a customer-support service; that observation inspires a shared checklist and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 033. Educational carousel × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A curious beginner wants to see how a process works; a demonstration of a learning program uses a short demonstration to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A service professional wants to use a new feature correctly; a demonstration of a community membership uses a real customer question to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 034. Educational carousel × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. An independent creator questions whether a subscription offer fits their needs; evidence such as an approved customer quote paired with its context provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A workshop maker questions whether a home-storage product fits their needs; evidence such as a comparison chart built from verified specifications provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 035. Educational carousel × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A student compares a beauty product with a do-it-yourself approach; a guide to time and setup helps explain which option is better suited to travel.
2. A local shop owner compares a specialty food item with a premium alternative; a guide to features the customer actually needs helps explain which option is better suited to a one-time project.

### 036. Educational carousel × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team manager starts with a service process that feels opaque; the story follows how a one-page comparison guide changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A returning customer starts with a launch message that assumes too much; the story follows how a transparent pricing breakdown changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 037. Educational carousel × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A support teammate shows one detail of a seasonal product; the maker uses a return-policy summary to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a content plan that changes every week.
2. A maker shows one detail of a subscription offer; the maker uses a side-by-side use case to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a search that returns too many choices.

### 038. Educational carousel × Identity and community

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of people comparing products, an independent creator shares a price they cannot evaluate; a visual label turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of small creative teams, a workshop maker shares a maintenance step they keep forgetting; a close-up product detail turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 039. Educational carousel × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a service package is introduced, the team previews a prototype review; a student gets a clear answer to a repeated manual task and knows what to expect.
2. Before a premium product is introduced, the team previews an annotated quick-start card; a local shop owner gets a clear answer to a purchase requiring multiple approvals and knows what to expect.

### 040. Educational carousel × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Use one idea per slide: hook, context, explanation, evidence, action. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a product-test comment from a team manager, the team revisits a reusable travel accessory; the team uses a mobile-page rewrite to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a creator note about the opening hook from a returning customer, the team revisits a seasonal product; the team uses a design mockup to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 5: Static story-led ad

### 041. Static story-led ad × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A community organizer considering a reusable travel accessory faces a search that returns too many choices; the story uses a one-page comparison guide to make one practical next step clear.
2. A curious beginner considering a seasonal product faces an unclear return or delivery policy; the story uses a transparent pricing breakdown to make one practical next step clear.

### 042. Static story-led ad × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a maintenance step they keep forgetting from a prospective client while they consider a creative service; that observation inspires a return-policy summary and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about uncertainty about which option suits them from an independent creator while they consider a digital planning tool; that observation inspires a side-by-side use case and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 043. Static story-led ad × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A small-business owner wants to maintain a product over time; a demonstration of a retail display uses a visual label to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A student wants to see how a process works; a demonstration of a service package uses a close-up product detail to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 044. Static story-led ad × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A busy parent questions whether an onboarding program fits their needs; evidence such as a documented test with its method provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A team manager questions whether a reusable travel accessory fits their needs; evidence such as an expert explanation with sources provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 045. Static story-led ad × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A community organizer compares an everyday apparel item with a low-cost option; a guide to repair and care helps explain which option is better suited to a shared workspace.
2. A curious beginner compares a creative service with a traditional service; a guide to the context where each works best helps explain which option is better suited to new-customer onboarding.

### 046. Static story-led ad × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A prospective client starts with a question they feel awkward asking; the story follows how clear care instructions change one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. An independent creator starts with a service process that feels opaque; the story follows how a sample or trial changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 047. Static story-led ad × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A maker shows one detail of a small-business software tool; the maker uses a live demo to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a routine they keep postponing.
2. A designer shows one detail of an onboarding program; the maker uses a short demonstration to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a content plan that changes every week.

### 048. Static story-led ad × Identity and community

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of first-time founders, a busy parent shares an unclear return or delivery policy; the group uses checklist to turn a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of independent makers, a team manager shares a price they cannot evaluate; a community Q&A turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 049. Static story-led ad × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a fitness accessory is introduced, the team previews a real customer question; a community organizer gets a clear answer to uncertainty about which option suits them and knows what to expect.
2. Before a personal-care product is introduced, the team previews an expert walkthrough; a curious beginner gets a clear answer to a repeated manual task and knows what to expect.

### 050. Static story-led ad × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Use one clear headline, one visual, and only a substantiated support point. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a poll about preferred packaging from a prospective client, the team revisits a customer-support service; the team uses a step-by-step sequence to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a review mentioning unclear sizing from an independent creator, the team revisits a small-business software tool; the team uses a one-page comparison guide to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 6: Customer testimonial interview

### 051. Customer testimonial interview × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A subscription member considering a customer-support service faces a content plan that changes every week; the story uses clear care instructions to make one practical next step clear.
2. A small-business owner considering a small-business software tool faces a search that returns too many choices; the story uses a sample or trial to make one practical next step clear.

### 052. Customer testimonial interview × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a price they cannot evaluate from a first-time shopper while they consider a community membership; that observation inspires a live demo and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about a maintenance step they keep forgetting from a busy parent while they consider a crafted home object; that observation inspires a short demonstration and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 053. Customer testimonial interview × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A new team member wants to compare materials and construction; a demonstration of a home-storage product uses a shared checklist to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A community organizer wants to maintain a product over time; a demonstration of a fitness accessory uses a community Q&A to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 054. Customer testimonial interview × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A product designer questions whether a specialty food item fits their needs; evidence such as a case example shared with permission provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A prospective client questions whether a customer-support service fits their needs; evidence such as a transparent demonstration under stated conditions provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 055. Customer testimonial interview × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A subscription member compares a learning program with a premium alternative; a guide to time and setup helps explain which option is better suited to a repeat routine.
2. A small-business owner compares a community membership with a manual workaround; a guide to features the customer actually needs helps explain which option is better suited to a gift purchase.

### 056. Customer testimonial interview × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A first-time shopper starts with a product claim without enough evidence; the story follows how a customer feedback session changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A busy parent starts with a question they feel awkward asking; the story follows how a return-policy summary changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

1. A designer shows one detail of a beauty product; the maker uses a transparent pricing breakdown to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a launch message that assumes too much.
2. A service lead shows one detail of a specialty food item; the maker uses a visual label to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a routine they keep postponing.

### 058. Customer testimonial interview × Identity and community

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of busy families, a product designer shares a search that returns too many choices; a side-by-side use case turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of design learners, a prospective client shares an unclear return or delivery policy; a prototype review turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 059. Customer testimonial interview × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a seasonal product is introduced, the team previews a close-up product detail; a subscription member gets a clear answer to a maintenance step they keep forgetting and knows what to expect.
2. Before a subscription offer is introduced, the team previews a mobile-page rewrite; a small-business owner gets a clear answer to uncertainty about which option suits them and knows what to expect.

### 060. Customer testimonial interview × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Ask open questions; retain the customer's own words, context, and permission. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

## Core format 7: Before-and-after sequence

### 061. Before-and-after sequence × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Show comparable starting and ending states, then identify what caused the difference. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A gift shopper considering a digital planning tool faces a routine they keep postponing; the story uses a customer feedback session to make one practical next step clear.
2. A new team member considering a beauty product faces a content plan that changes every week; the story uses a return-policy summary to make one practical next step clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a small-business software tool is introduced, the team previews a community Q&A; a gift shopper gets a clear answer to a price they cannot evaluate and knows what to expect.
2. Before an onboarding program is introduced, the team previews a step-by-step sequence; a new team member gets a clear answer to a maintenance step they keep forgetting and knows what to expect.

### 070. Before-and-after sequence × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Show comparable starting and ending states, then identify what caused the difference. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a repeated help-center search from a retail associate, the team revisits a crafted home object; the team uses an expert walkthrough to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a return reason from a product designer, the team revisits an everyday apparel item; the team uses a customer feedback session to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 8: Customer case study

### 071. Customer case study × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A returning customer considering a crafted home object faces a launch message that assumes too much; the story uses a design mockup to make one practical next step clear.
2. A commuter considering an everyday apparel item faces a routine they keep postponing; the story uses a live demo to make one practical next step clear.

### 072. Customer case study × Origin and purpose

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Show the observation or need that sparked the brand, product, or campaign.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A team hears about a search that returns too many choices from a service professional while they consider a fitness accessory; that observation inspires a sample or trial and clarifies why the offer exists.
2. A team hears about an unclear return or delivery policy from a cautious buyer while they consider a personal-care product; that observation inspires a shared checklist and clarifies why the offer exists.

### 073. Customer case study × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A workshop maker wants to decide what to ask before buying; a demonstration of a customer-support service uses a short demonstration to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A gift shopper wants to reduce uncertainty around an order; a demonstration of a small-business software tool uses a real customer question to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 074. Customer case study × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A local shop owner questions whether a community membership fits their needs; evidence such as published product details and limitations provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A retail associate questions whether a crafted home object fits their needs; evidence such as reviews that include both strengths and tradeoffs provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 075. Customer case study × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A returning customer compares a home-storage product with a manual workaround; a guide to time and setup helps explain which option is better suited to new-customer onboarding.
2. A commuter compares a fitness accessory with a one-size-fits-all package; a guide to features the customer actually needs helps explain which option is better suited to a daily commute.

### 076. Customer case study × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service professional starts with a mismatch between expectations and reality; the story follows how a one-page comparison guide changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A cautious buyer starts with a setup process with too many steps; the story follows how a transparent pricing breakdown changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 077. Customer case study × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A product team shows one detail of a learning program; the maker uses a return-policy summary to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a question they feel awkward asking.
2. A support teammate shows one detail of a community membership; the maker uses a side-by-side use case to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a service process that feels opaque.

### 078. Customer case study × Identity and community

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of independent makers, a local shop owner shares a routine they keep postponing; a visual label turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of returning customers, a retail associate shares a content plan that changes every week; a close-up product detail turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 079. Customer case study × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a beauty product is introduced, the team previews a prototype review; a returning customer gets a clear answer to an unclear return or delivery policy and knows what to expect.
2. Before a specialty food item is introduced, the team previews an annotated quick-start card; a commuter gets a clear answer to a price they cannot evaluate and knows what to expect.

### 080. Customer case study × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Structure it as challenge, decision, process, evidence, and lesson. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a team member's workflow note from a service professional, the team revisits a premium product; the team uses a mobile-page rewrite to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a support question about the first step from a cautious buyer, the team revisits a learning program; the team uses a design mockup to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 14: Podcast or interview clip

### 131. Podcast or interview clip × Problem-to-solution

## Core format 20: Product-page narrative

### 191. Product-page narrative × Problem-to-solution

**Execution:** Order the page from need to benefit, details, proof, limits, and purchase action. Frame the story around a real friction, a practical response, and a clear next step.

## Core format 21: Street interview

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A retail associate questions whether a home-storage product fits their needs; evidence such as photographs showing the actual finish provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A product designer questions whether a fitness accessory fits their needs; evidence such as a clear returns policy and support path provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 365. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A commuter compares a specialty food item with a traditional service; a guide to repair and care helps explain which option is better suited to a one-time project.
2. A subscription member compares a customer-support service with a familiar routine; a guide to the context where each works best helps explain which option is better suited to a small team.

### 366. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A cautious buyer starts with a maintenance step they keep forgetting; the story follows how clear care instructions change one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A first-time shopper starts with uncertainty about which option suits them; the story follows how a sample or trial changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 367. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service lead shows one detail of a subscription offer; the maker uses a live demo to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a purchase requiring multiple approvals.
2. A product team shows one detail of a home-storage product; the maker uses a short demonstration to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a page that hides key details.

### 368. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Identity and community

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of small creative teams, a retail associate shares conflicting advice; the group uses checklist to turn a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of customers learning a new skill, a product designer shares a lack of confidence in the first step; a community Q&A turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 369. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a premium product is introduced, the team previews a real customer question; a commuter gets a clear answer to a mismatch between expectations and reality and knows what to expect.
2. Before a learning program is introduced, the team previews an expert walkthrough; a subscription member gets a clear answer to a setup process with too many steps and knows what to expect.

### 370. Seasonal or cultural-moment campaign × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Use the moment only when it genuinely fits the customer and brand. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.

**Short-story examples:**

1. After a repeated help-center search from a cautious buyer, the team revisits a seasonal product; the team uses a step-by-step sequence to show what changed and what the team learned.
2. After a return reason from a first-time shopper, the team revisits a subscription offer; the team uses a one-page comparison guide to show what changed and what the team learned.

## Core format 38: Challenge or contest activation

### 371. Challenge or contest activation × Problem-to-solution

1. A workshop maker considering a seasonal product faces a page that hides key details; the story uses clear care instructions to make one practical next step clear.
2. A gift shopper considering a subscription offer faces a crowded shelf with no comparison cues; the story uses a sample or trial to make one practical next step clear.

### 423. One-shot narrative video × Mechanism and how it works

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Explain the steps, design, or service process that makes the offer useful.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A local shop owner wants to use a new feature correctly; a demonstration of an everyday apparel item uses a side-by-side use case to show how the process works and where it may fit.
2. A retail associate wants to make a more informed choice; a demonstration of a creative service uses a prototype review to show how the process works and where it may fit.

### 424. One-shot narrative video × Objection and proof

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Start with a reasonable concern and answer it with relevant, verified evidence.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A returning customer questions whether a personal-care product fits their needs; evidence such as reviews that include both strengths and tradeoffs provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.
2. A commuter questions whether a retail display fits their needs; evidence such as published product details and limitations provides context and makes the limits of the claim clear.

### 425. One-shot narrative video × Comparison and tradeoff

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Help the audience compare options against consistent criteria and use cases.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A service professional compares a small-business software tool with a quick fix; a guide to repair and care helps explain which option is better suited to a busy season.
2. A cautious buyer compares an onboarding program with a low-cost option; a guide to the context where each works best helps explain which option is better suited to a repeat routine.

### 426. One-shot narrative video × Transformation and journey

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Follow a before, turning point, and documented change without exaggerating outcomes.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A workshop maker starts with a launch message that assumes too much; the story follows how a design mockup changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.
2. A gift shopper starts with a routine they keep postponing; the story follows how a live demo changes one step in their routine, with any result shown only if verified.

### 427. One-shot narrative video × Craft and detail

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Reveal the design, materials, decisions, or process behind the finished experience.

**Short-story examples:**

1. A product team shows one detail of a fitness accessory; the maker uses a sample or trial to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses a search that returns too many choices.
2. A support teammate shows one detail of a personal-care product; the maker uses a shared checklist to reveal the decisions behind the finish and addresses an unclear return or delivery policy.

### 428. One-shot narrative video × Identity and community

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Connect the story to shared values, routines, or a community people can recognize.

**Short-story examples:**

1. In a community of people building better routines, a returning customer shares a maintenance step they keep forgetting; a short demonstration turns a solo question into a practical conversation.
2. In a community of busy families, a commuter shares uncertainty about which option suits them; a real customer question turns a solo question into a practical conversation.

### 429. One-shot narrative video × Launch, offer, and timing

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Build anticipation around a relevant launch or offer while keeping terms and timing clear.

**Short-story examples:**

1. Before a community membership is introduced, the team previews a community Q&A; a service professional gets a clear answer to a purchase requiring multiple approvals and knows what to expect.
2. Before a crafted home object is introduced, the team previews a step-by-step sequence; a cautious buyer gets a clear answer to a page that hides key details and knows what to expect.

### 430. One-shot narrative video × Feedback and iteration

**Execution:** Plan the movement so the action itself explains the story. Show how a question, test, or response changed the next version and what was learned.
